It left me with a pretty serious back injury, and honestly, everything just started unraveling after that. That's when this whole thing began.\n\nDoctor: Since that fall 14 months ago, has this heavy, hopeless feeling been pretty much constant, or have there been stretches of time — maybe days or weeks — where you felt noticeably better?\n\nPatient: It's been pretty constant, honestly. I haven't really had any stretches where I felt... noticeably better. It just kind of set in after the fall and, well, it hasn't lifted since.\n\nDoctor: When you think about your heavy, hopeless feelings over this past year — do they seem to come and go based on how your back is doing or what's happening with your recovery, or do they feel pretty independent of that?\n\nPatient: I don't really know. I guess... I mean, my back is always hurting to some degree, so it's hard to separate the two. But even on days where the pain isn't quite as sharp, or I'm just resting on the couch, that heavy feeling is still just... there. It doesn't seem to matter what's going on with my physical recovery anymore. It's just a constant weight.\n\nDoctor: When you had that fall 14 months ago, did the heavy, hopeless feeling start right away — like in the days immediately after — or did it take a few weeks or longer to develop?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was saying, it's been going on for about a year and two months now. I guess it started pretty much right away. Once I had that fall at the HVAC job and got the back injury... it was like everything just started unraveling right then. So yeah, it's been there ever since it happened.\n\nDoctor: If your back had healed completely and you were back to your normal physical condition, do you think this heavy, hopeless feeling would go away, or do you think it would still be there?\n\nPatient: I... I really don't know. It's hard to even picture what it would be like to have my back totally healed at this point. I just know that right then is when everything started unraveling, and it's been like this ever since.\n\nDoctor: When you think back to that first week after the fall — in those first few days, did you wake up feeling that heavy, hopeless feeling right away, or did it take several days to a week or so before it really settled in?\n\nPatient: Doctor, I... I just know it started then, and I've been stuck in it ever since.\n\nDoctor: In that first week after the fall, would you say the heavy hopeless feeling came on suddenly — like you felt it strongly within the first day or two — or did it build up more gradually over several days?\n\nPatient: Look, I really don't know how to answer that any differently than I already have. I mean, I've had bad days before, sure, like anyone else. But... nothing like this. I've never had a time where I just couldn't snap out of it for weeks on end. This is really the first time it's ever been this heavy.\n\nDoctor: Did anything specific happen — like a loss, a major change in your life, or a stressful event — around the time these heavy feelings started?\n\nPatient: Yeah, actually. It started about a year and two months ago. I work in HVAC installation and repair, and... well, I had a bad fall on a job. It left me with a pretty serious back injury, and honestly, everything just started unraveling after that. That's when this whole thing began.\n\nDoctor: Since that fall 14 months ago, has this heavy, hopeless feeling been pretty much constant, or have there been stretches of time — maybe days or weeks — where you felt noticeably better?\n\nPatient: It's been pretty constant, honestly. I haven't really had any stretches where I felt... noticeably better. It just kind of set in after the fall and, well, it hasn't lifted since.\n\nDoctor: When you think about your heavy, hopeless feelings over this past year — do they seem to come and go based on how your back is doing or what's happening with your recovery, or do they feel pretty independent of that?\n\nPatient: I don't really know. I guess... I mean, my back is always hurting to some degree, so it's hard to separate the two. But even on days where the pain isn't quite as sharp, or I'm just resting on the couch, that heavy feeling is still just... there. It doesn't seem to matter what's going on with my physical recovery anymore. It's just a constant weight.\n\nDoctor: When you had that fall 14 months ago, did the heavy, hopeless feeling start right away — like in the days immediately after — or did it take a few weeks or longer to develop?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was saying, it's been going on for about a year and two months now. I guess it started pretty much right away. Once I had that fall at the HVAC job and got the back injury... it was like everything just started unraveling right then. That's really all I can tell you about when it started.\n\n[Last Question]\nWhen you think back to that fall and the days right after it happened — in those first few days, before you even had time to think about long-term recovery or missing work, did you feel that heavy, hopeless feeling right then in your body and mind, or did it only start to develop once you realized you couldn't work?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 49 / installation_maintenance_or_repair_worker / high_school\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Duration\nmanifestation: \"This has been going on for about a year and two months, ever since the fall at the HVAC job left me with the back injury and everything started unraveling.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Plain patient.\nA plain patient describes their symptoms and experiences in a direct and straightforward manner, providing clear and relevant answers to the doctor's questions.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Doc, I... I don't know how many different ways to answer this. You're asking me the exact same thing again, and I really don't have a different answer for you. I just don't remember those first couple of days. Like I've been saying, it's been about a year and two months of this. I took that fall at the HVAC job, got this back injury, and that's when everything just started unraveling. I really can't break it down any more than that.

  "diagnosis": "F43.21",

  "reason": "The patient presents with persistent depressed mood (heavy, hopeless feeling) that began immediately following a significant psychosocial stressor (work-related back injury 14 months ago) and has remained constant since. The symptomatology meets criteria for Adjustment Disorder with Depressed Mood (F43.21) rather than Major Depressive Disorder, as: (1) the onset is clearly linked to an identifiable stressor, (2) symptoms began shortly after the stressor, (3) the patient acknowledges uncertainty about whether symptoms would persist if the stressor were resolved, and (4) there is no evidence of prior depressive episodes. The temporal relationship between the stressor and symptom onset, combined with the patient's inability to fully differentiate the mood symptoms from the ongoing physical consequences of the injury, supports an adjustment disorder diagnosis.",

          "Constant nature of the mood disturbance for 14 months"
        ],
        "count": 3

}
